President’s Letter Gérard Fitoussi
As I write this president’s letter, summer is st i l l wi th us. In France, we've just experienced an extraordinary two weeks with the Olympic Games, which captivated the world and proved that the worst is not always inevitable. 
 
Long before the Games began, many p e s s i m i s t s p r e d i c t e d i s s u e s w i t h organisation, logistics, and transportation, some even warning of potential "security disaster." Yet, none of these problems materialised, and we had the privilege of being part of an incredible adventure. What thwarted these negative predictions was careful planning, hard work, tenacity, attention to detail, and a belief in the future, in humanity, and in our capabilities. Thanks to everyone who prepared for these Games, success was achieved—a remarkable lesson for us all. 
 
When embarking on any venture, there are always those who focus only on the negatives, the obstacles, the dangers, and who share their fears and concerns. And to some extent, they are right; one should not plunge headfirst into an adventure without preparation. It's important to take the time to prepare, to know where you want to go, and then to gather the necessary resources and have confidence. But once that is done, it’s crucial to move forward without hesitation. 
 
Similarly, as healthcare professionals, when a patient consults us, we must work with them to determine their goals, paying close attention to details, their words, and gestures, and then use all the resources at our disposal to help them achieve those goals. Even when situations seem hopeless, we must not give in to despair, but instead maintain hope and communicate it to the patient—a reasonable hope that healing is possible, or at the very least, that improvement is within reach. 
 
Of all Milton Erickson's qualities, the most remarkable and influential was his ability to maintain and convey hope to those who sought his help. Erickson had personally experienced this hope when, as a young man afflicted by polio, he embarked on a solo canoe journey for several months. He knew he could do it, had prepared his journey, planned his destination and stages, and disregarded the fears and warnings he received. 
 
For the ESH, we continue to work on welcoming new associations, some from countries that already have hypnosis institutes, and others that will bring new countries into the ESH, further solidifying our position as the leading European association.   
 
The joint project with the ISH, "Hypnosis Without Borders" (HWB), is progressing well. We are currently working with Kathleen Long, the project’s president, on the final administrative steps and beginning to consider possible actions. 
 
The research project, ESHRP 2024-2026, led by Randi Abrahamsen and initiated with Peter Naish, is also on track. I encourage you to inform your institutes and members about this unique project, which focuses on the practice of hypnosis, not in laboratories or hospital settings, but in each of our private practices. This is a tremendous opportunity for us all to participate in a project with simple guidelines. If each of our thousands of members includes just one or two patients, we could gather significant results from tens of thousands of data points. Please consider participating, as the stakes are high. 
 
I’m pleased to announce that Andreas Kollar will head the CEPE committee. 
 
I would like to express my gratitude to Randi  Abrahamsen  for her dedicated work as the  head of CEPE, she will be able to focus on  the Research committee 
 
I am also pleased to confirm the upcoming release of a special issue of the International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis dedicated to research conducted by European teams. This project took shape  
 
following the European Congress in Antalya, Turkey, in October 2023. 
 
It is a great source of pride to witness how vibrant and dynamic European research is in the field of hypnosis. Europe is establishing itself as a key player. The diversity of talent, cross-border collaboration, and the variety of projects are enabling European research to push the boundaries of knowledge. This is a true source of inspiration and optimism for the future. 
 
A special thanks to Gary Elkins, the editor of the International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, and to Peter Naish, who has been instrumental in bringing this wonderful project to life. Gary will outline the key points of this special issue in this newsletter. 
 
Additionally, this newsletter is yours, and through our institutes, it reaches out thousands of members. Please circulate it and share it widely. You are also welcome to contribute to it if you wish; in that case, feel free to contact Kathleen Long. 
 
I will end by expressing my hope that this summer has allowed you to recharge and that you can start this new season with strength and energy. 
 
 
 
Translated into French by Gérard Fitoussi 
Au moment où j'écris cette lettre en tant que président, l'été est encore là. En France, nous venons de vivre quinze jours extraordinaires avec les Jeux olympiques qui ont enchanté le monde et ont démontré que le pire n'est pas toujours assuré de survenir. Bien avant le début des jeux, combien de Cassandre ont prédit la survenue de problèmes d'organisation, de logistique, de transport et de logistique, certains ayant même évoqué une « catastrophe sécuritaire » ? Rien de tout cela ne s'est produit et nous avons eu la chance de participer à une aventure incroyable. Ce qui a déjoué toutes ces prévisions négatives, c'est, la planification, le travail, la ténacité, l'attention aux détails, ainsi que la foi en l’avenir et en l’homme. Grâce à tous ceux qui ont préparé ces jeux, le succès a été au rendez-vous. Une formidable leçon pour nous tous. Lorsque nous entreprenons une aventure, quelle qu'elle soit, nombreux sont ceux qui n'en perçoivent que le côté négatif, les obstacles, les dangers et communiquent leurs peurs et leurs craintes. Et ils ont en partie raison. Il ne faut pas se jeter tête baissée dans l’aventure, mais prendre le temps de se préparer, de savoir où l'on veut aller puis de s’en donner les moyens et avoir confiance. Mais cela fait, ensuite il ne faut pas hésiter et aller de l’avant. Il en est de même, pour nous, professionnels de santé, lorsqu’un patient nous consulte, nous devons déterminer avec lui où il veut aller, quel est son but, être attentif aux détails, à ses mots, ses gestes, et mettre ensuite en œuvre tous les moyens dont nous disposons pour l'aider à y parvenir. Et même si certaines situations nous semblent sans issue, ne pas se résigner, garder l'espoir et le communiquer au patient. L’espoir raisonnable qu'une guérison est possible ou à tout le moins qu'une amélioration est envisageable. De toutes les qualités de Milton Erickson, la plus folle et la plus déterminante était sa capacité de garder et de transmettre l'espoir à celles et ceux qui le consultaient.  Et cet espoir, il l'avait lui-même expérimenté, lorsque jeune homme atteint de poliomyélite, il avait voyagé seul sur un canoé pendant plusieurs mois. Il savait qu'il pouvait le faire, et avait préparé son voyage, sa destination, ses étapes et ne tint pas compte des peurs et des avertissements reçus .
 
Pour l’ESH, nous continuons à travailler pour accueillir de nouvelles associations, certaines venant de pays ayant déjà des instituts d'hypnose, et d'autres qui vont ajouter à l'ESH de nouveaux pays, confirmant ainsi notre position de première association européenne.
 
Le projet mené en commun avec l’ISH, Hypnosis Without Borders (HWB), avance bien, nous procédons avec Kathleen Long, la présidente, 
aux dernières étapes administratives et commençons à réfléchir aux modalités d’actions possibles. Le projet de recherche, ESHRP 2024-2026, dirigé par Randi Abrahamsen et initié avec Peter Naish, est lui aussi en bonne voie. Aussi, je vous invite à en informer chacun de vos instituts et de vos membres. Il s'agit d'un projet unique en son genre puisqu'il porte sur la pratique de l'hypnose, non pas en laboratoire ou dans des services hospitaliers, mais dans les cabinets de chacun d'entre nous, en pratique de ville. C'est une formidable opportunité pour nous tous  de pouvoir participer à ce projet dont les modalités sont simples. Chacun peut en être acteur. Et si chacun de nos milliers de membres inclut ne serait-ce qu'un ou deux patients, nous pourrions obtenir des résultats considérables sur plus d'une dizaine de milliers de données. Merci de prendre le temps d'envisager votre participation, car les enjeux sont considérables. 
 
Je suis heureux d'annoncer qu'Andreas Kollar dirigera le comité du CEPE. J'aimerais exprimer ma gratitude à Randi Abrahamsen pour son travail dévoué à la tête du CEPE, qui lui permettra de se concentrer sur le comité de recherche. Par ailleurs, j’ai le plaisir de confirmer la sortie prochaine d’un numéro spécial de l’International Journal of Clinical and Expérimental Hypnosis dédié à la recherche menée par des équipes européennes. Ce projet qui a vu le jour après le congrès européen à Antalya en Turquie en octobre 2023. 
 
C’est une immense fierté de constater à quel point la recherche européenne est vibrante et dynamique dans le domaine de l’hypnose. L'Europe s'affirme comme un acteur clé. La diversité des talents, la coopération transfrontalière et la diversité des projets permettent à la recherche européenne de repousser les frontières du savoir. C’est une véritable source d'inspiration et d'optimisme pour l'avenir. Merci à Gary Elkins, l’éditeur de l’International Journal of Clinical and Expérimental Hypnosis, et à Peter Naish qui ont été à la manœuvre de ce beau projet. Gary détaillera les grandes lignes de ce numéro dans cette newsletter.
 
Par ailleurs, cette newsletter est la vôtre, et au travers de nos instituts, celle de nos milliers de membres, faites-la circuler et lire autour de vous. Vous pouvez aussi y contribuer si vous le souhaitez et pour cela n’hésitez pas à contacter Kathleen Long. Je terminerai simplement en espérant que cet été vous aura permis de vous ressourcer et d’entamer cette rentrée avec force et énergie.
 
Translated into German  by Stella Nkenke 
Während ich diesen Brief des Präsidenten schreibe, ist der Sommer noch nicht vorbei . In Frankreich haben wir gerade zwei außergewöhnliche Wochen mit den Olympischen Spielen erlebt, die die Welt in ihren Bann gezogen und bewiesen haben, dass das Schlimmste nicht immer unvermeidlich ist. Lange vor Beginn der Spiele sagten viele Pessimisten Probleme mit der Organisation, der Logistik und dem Transport voraus, einige warnten sogar vor einer möglichen „Sicherheitskatastrophe“. Doch keines dieser Probleme trat ein, und wir hatten das Privileg, Teil eines unglaublichen Abenteuers zu sein. Was diese negativen Vorhersagen zunichte machte, waren sorgfältige Planung, harte Arbeit, Hartnäckigkeit, Liebe zum Detail und der Glaube an die Zukunft, an die Menschheit und an unsere Fähigkeiten. Dank aller, die sich auf diese Spiele vorbereitet haben, wurde der Erfolg erzielt - eine bemerkenswerte Lektion für uns alle. Wenn man sich auf ein Vorhaben einlässt, gibt es immer diejenigen, die sich nur auf das Negative, die Hindernisse und die Gefahren konzentrieren und ihre Ängste und Bedenken äußern. Und bis zu einem gewissen Grad haben sie Recht: Man sollte sich nicht kopfüber in ein Abenteuer stürzen, ohne sich vorzubereiten. Es ist wichtig, sich die Zeit zu nehmen, sich vorzubereiten, zu wissen, wohin man gehen will, und dann die notwendigen Ressourcen zu sammeln und Vertrauen zu haben. Sobald dies jedoch geschehen ist, ist es wichtig, ohne Zögern voranzuschreiten. In ähnlicher Weise müssen wir als Angehörige der Gesundheitsberufe, wenn ein Patient uns konsultiert, mit ihm zusammenarbeiten, um seine Ziele zu bestimmen, wobei wir genau auf Details, seine Worte und Gesten achten müssen, und dann alle uns zur Verfügung stehenden Ressourcen nutzen, um ihm zu helfen, diese Ziele zu erreichen. Selbst in aussichtslos erscheinenden Situationen dürfen wir nicht in Verzweiflung verfallen, sondern müssen die Hoffnung aufrechterhalten und sie dem Patienten vermitteln - eine begründete Hoffnung, dass Heilung möglich ist oder dass zumindest eine Verbesserung in Reichweite liegt. Von allen Qualitäten Milton Ericksons war die bemerkenswerteste und einflussreichste seine Fähigkeit, denjenigen, die seine Hilfe suchten, Hoffnung zu geben und zu vermitteln. Erickson hatte diese Hoffnung am eigenen Leib erfahren, als er als junger Mann, der an Kinderlähmung erkrankt war, zu einer mehrmonatigen Kanufahrt aufbrach. Er wusste, dass er es schaffen konnte, hatte seine Reise vorbereitet, sein Ziel und seine Etappen geplant und die Ängste und Warnungen, die er erhielt, außer Acht gelassen. Für die ESH arbeiten wir weiter daran, neue Verbände aufzunehmen, einige aus Ländern, die bereits Hypnoseinstitute haben, und andere, die neue Länder in die ESH bringen werden, um unsere Position als führender europäischer Verband weiter zu festigen. Das gemeinsame Projekt mit der ISH, "Hypnosis Without Borders" (HWB), kommt gut voran. Wir arbeiten derzeit mit Kathleen Long, der Präsidentin 
des Projekts, an den letzten administrativen Schritten und beginnen, mögliche Maßnahmen in Betracht zu ziehen.
 
Das von Randi Abrahamsen geleitete und gemeinsam mit Peter Naish initiierte Forschungsprojekt ESHRP 2024-2026 ist ebenfalls auf gutem Wege. Ich möchte Sie ermutigen, Ihre Institute und Mitglieder über dieses einzigartige Projekt zu informieren, das sich auf die Praxis der Hypnose konzentriert, und zwar nicht im  Labor oder Krankenhaus, sondern in jeder unserer privaten Praxen. Dies ist eine großartige Gelegenheit für uns alle, an einem Projekt mit einfachen Leitlinien teilzunehmen. Wenn jedes unserer Tausenden von Mitgliedern nur einen oder zwei Patienten einschließt, könnten wir aus Zehntausenden von Datenpunkten aussagekräftige Ergebnisse gewinnen. Bitte denken Sie über eine Teilnahme nach, denn es steht viel auf dem Spiel. Ich freue mich, Ihnen mitteilen zu können, dass Andreas Kollar den CEPE-Ausschuss ab jetzt leiten wird. Ich möchte Randi Abrahamsen für ihre jahrelange und engagierte Arbeit als Leiterin des CEPE-Ausschusses danken. Sie wird sich jetzt  vorwiegend auf den Forschungsausschuss konzentrieren. Ich freue mich auch, die bevorstehende Veröffentlichung einer Sonderausgabe des International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis bestätigen zu können, die der Forschung europäischer Teams gewidmet ist. Dieses Projekt wurde im Anschluss an den Europäischen Kongress in Antalya, Türkei, im Oktober 2023 ins Leben gerufen.
 
Es erfüllt uns mit Stolz zu sehen, wie lebendig und dynamisch die europäische Forschung auf dem Gebiet der Hypnose ist. Europa ist dabei, sich als wichtiger Akteur zu etablieren. Die Vielfalt der Talente, die grenzüberschreitende Zusammenarbeit und die Vielfalt der Projekte ermöglichen es der europäischen Forschung, die Grenzen des Wissens zu erweitern. Dies ist eine wahre Quelle der Inspiration und des Optimismus für die Zukunft. Ein besonderer Dank gilt Gary Elkins, dem Herausgeber des International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, und Peter Naish, die maßgeblich dazu beigetragen haben, dieses wunderbare Projekt ins Leben zu rufen. Gary wird die wichtigsten Punkte dieser Sonderausgabe in diesem Newsletter erläutern.Außerdem gehört dieser Newsletter Ihnen, und über unsere Institute erreicht er Tausende von Mitgliedern. Bitte leiten Sie ihn weiter. Wenn Sie einen Beitrag leisten möchten, können Sie sich gerne an Kathleen Long wenden. Abschließend möchte ich meine Hoffnung zum Ausdruck bringen, dass Sie in diesem Sommer neue Kraft und Energie schöpfen konnten und dass Sie die neue Saison mit Stärke und Energie beginnen können.
 
Translated into Spanish                  by José Cava 
Mientras escribo esta carta del presidente, el verano todavía está entre nosotros. En Francia, acabamos de vivir dos semanas extraordinarias con los Juegos Olímpicos, que cautivaron al mundo y demostraron que lo peor no siempre es inevitable. Mucho antes de que comenzaran los Juegos, muchos pesimistas predijeron problemas con la organización, la logística y el transporte, algunos incluso advirtieron de un posible "desastre de seguridad". Sin embargo, ninguno de estos problemas se materializó y tuvimos el privilegio de ser parte de una aventura increíble. Lo que frustró estas predicciones negativas fue la planificación cuidadosa, el trabajo duro, la tenacidad, la atención al detalle y la fe en el futuro, en la humanidad y en nuestras capacidades. Gracias a todos los que se prepararon para estos Juegos, se logró el éxito: una lección notable para todos nosotros. Al embarcarse en cualquier aventura, siempre hay quienes se centran solo en los aspectos negativos, los obstáculos, los pel igros y comparten sus miedos y preocupaciones. Y hasta cierto punto, tienen razón: uno no debe lanzarse de cabeza a una aventura sin preparación. Es importante tomarse el tiempo para prepararse, saber a dónde se quiere llegar y luego reunir los recursos necesarios y tener confianza. Pero una vez hecho eso, es crucial avanzar sin dudarlo. De la misma manera, como profesionales de la salud, cuando un paciente nos consulta, debemos trabajar con él para determinar sus objetivos, prestando mucha atención a los detalles, a sus palabras y gestos, y luego utilizar todos los recursos a nuestra disposición para ayudarlo a lograr esos objetivos. Incluso cuando las situaciones parecen desesperadas, no debemos ceder a la desesperación, sino mantener la esperanza y comunicársela al paciente: una esperanza razonable de que la curación es posible, o al menos, de que la mejoría es alcanzable. De todas las cualidades de Milton Erickson, la más notable e influyente fue su capacidad para mantener y transmitir esperanza a quienes buscaban su ayuda. Erickson había experimentado personalmente esta esperanza cuando, siendo un joven afligido por la polio, se embarcó en un viaje en canoa en solitario durante varios meses. Sabía que podía hacerlo, había preparado su viaje, planeado el destino y las etapas, y no hizo caso de los miedos y advertencias que recibió. En la ESH, seguimos trabajando para dar la bienvenida a nuevas asociaciones, algunas de países que ya cuentan con institutos de hipnosis, y otras que traerán nuevos países a la ESH, consolidando aún más nuestra posición como la asociación líder europea. El proyecto conjunto con la ISH, "Hipnosis Without Borders" (HWB), avanza bien. Actualmente estamos trabajando 
con Kathleen Long, la presidenta del proyecto, en los últimos pasos administrativos y comenzando a considerar posibles acciones. El proyecto de investigación, ESHRP 2024-2026, dirigido por Randi Abrahamsen e iniciado con Peter Naish, también está en marcha. Los animo a informar a sus institutos y miembros sobre este proyecto único, que se centra en la práctica de la hipnosis, no en laboratorios o entornos hospitalarios, sino en cada una de nuestras prácticas privadas. Esta es una gran oportunidad para que todos participemos en un proyecto con pautas simples. Si cada uno de nuestros miles de miembros incluyera tan solo uno o dos pacientes, podríamos obtener resultados significativos a partir de decenas de miles de puntos de datos. Considere su participación, por favor, ya que hay mucho en juego. Me complace anunciar que Andreas Kollar dirigirá el comité CEPE. Me gustaría expresar mi gratitud a Randi Abrahamsen por su dedicación como directora del CEPE, ahora podrá centrarse en el comité de investigación.
 
También me complace confirmar el próximo lanzamiento de un número especial de la Revista Internacional de Hipnosis Clínica y Experimental dedicado a la investigación realizada por equipos europeos. Este proyecto tomó forma tras el Congreso Europeo en Antalya, Turquía, en octubre de 2023. Es un gran motivo de orgullo presenciar cuán vibrante y dinámica es la investigación europea en el campo de la hipnosis. Europa se está consolidando como un actor clave. La diversidad de talentos, la colaboración transfronteriza y la variedad de proyectos están permitiendo que la investigación europea amplíe los límites del conocimiento. Esta es una verdadera fuente de inspiración y optimismo para el futuro.Un agradecimiento especial a Gary Elkins, editor de la Revista Internacional de Hipnosis Clínica y Experimental, y a Peter Naish, quienes han sido fundamentales para dar vida a este maravilloso proyecto. Gary describirá los puntos clave de este número especial en este boletín.
 
Además, este boletín es vuestro y, a través de nuestros institutos, llega a miles de miembros. Os pido que lo difundáis y compartáis ampliamente. También sois bienvenidos a colaborar si lo deseáis; en ese caso, no dudéis en poneros en contacto con Kathleen Long. Terminaré expresando mi deseo de que este verano os haya permitido recargar las pilas y que podáis empezar esta nueva temporada con fuerza y energía.
Translated into Italian                      by Fabio Carnevale 
Mentre scrivo questa "lettera del presidente", l'estate è ancora con noi. In Francia, abbiamo appena vissuto due settimane straordinarie con i Giochi Olimpici, che hanno affascinato il mondo, dimostrando che il peggio non è sempre inevitabile.
 
Molto prima che i Giochi iniziassero, molti pessimisti avevano previsto problemi con l'organizzazione, la logistica e i trasporti, alcuni addirittura avvisando di un potenziale "disastro per la sicurezza". Eppure, nessuno di questi problemi si è materializzato e abbiamo avuto il privilegio di far parte di un'avventura incredibile. Ciò che ha sventato queste previsioni negative è stata un'attenta pianificazione, il duro lavoro, la tenacia, l'attenzione ai dettagli e la fiducia nel futuro, nell'umanità e nelle nostre capacità. Grazie a tutti coloro che si sono preparati per questi Giochi, il successo è stato raggiunto: una lezione straordinaria per tutti noi.
 
Quando si intraprende un'impresa, non mancano mai coloro che puntano solo sugli aspetti negativi, sugli ostacoli, sui pericoli e che condividono le proprie paure e preoccupazioni. E in una certa misura, hanno ragione: non ci si dovrebbe tuffare a capofitto in un'avventura senza preparazione. È importante prendersi il tempo per prepararsi, sapere dove si vuole andare, quindi raccogliere le risorse necessarie e avere fiducia. Ma una volta fatto questo, è fondamentale andare avanti senza esitazione. Allo stesso modo, come professionisti sanitari, quando un paziente ci consulta, dobbiamo lavorare con lui per determinare i suoi obiettivi, prestando molta attenzione ai dettagli, alle sue parole e ai suoi gesti, e quindi utilizzare tutte le risorse a nostra disposizione per aiutarlo a raggiungere quegli obiettivi. Anche quando le situazioni sembrano senza speranza, non dobbiamo cedere alla disperazione, ma piuttosto mantenere la speranza e comunicarla al paziente, una ragionevole speranza che la guarigione sia possibile o, per lo meno, che il miglioramento sia a portata di mano. Di tutte le qualità di Milton Erickson, la più notevole e influente era la sua capacità di mantenere e trasmettere speranza a coloro che cercavano il suo aiuto. Erickson aveva sperimentato personalmente questa speranza quando, da giovane affetto da poliomielite, intraprese un viaggio in canoa da solo per diversi mesi. Sapeva di potercela fare, aveva preparato il suo viaggio, pianificato la sua destinazione e le sue tappe, e ignorato le paure e gli avvertimenti ricevuti.
 
Per l'ESH, continuiamo a lavorare per accogliere nuove associazioni, alcune provenienti da paesi che hanno già istituti di ipnosi, e altre che porteranno nuovi paesi nell'ESH, consolidando ulteriormente la nostra 
posizione di principale associazione europea. Il progetto congiunto con l'ISH, "Hypnosis Without Borders" (HWB), sta procedendo bene. Stiamo attualmente lavorando con Kathleen Long, presidente del progetto, sugli ultimi passaggi amministrativi e stiamo iniziando a considerare possibili iniziative. Anche il progetto di ricerca, ESHRP 2024-2026, guidato da Randi Abrahamsen e avviato con Peter Naish, è sulla buona strada. Vi incoraggio a informare i vostri istituti e membri delle Società di questo progetto unico, che si concentra sulla pratica dell'ipnosi, non in laboratori o ospedali, ma in ciascuno dei nostri studi privati. Questa è una straordinaria opportunità per tutti noi di partecipare a un progetto con linee guida semplici. Se ognuno dei nostri migliaia di membri includesse solo uno o due pazienti, potremmo raccogliere risultati significativi attraverso decine di migliaia di dati. Vi prego di considerare la possibilità di partecipare, poiché la posta in gioco è alta.
 
Sono lieto di annunciare che Andreas Kollar guiderà il comitato CEPE. Vorrei esprimere la mia gratitudine a Randi Abrahamsen per la sua dedizione come responsabile del CEPE, che le consentirà di concentrarsi sul comitato di ricerca.
 
Sono inoltre lieto di confermare l'imminente uscita di un numero speciale dell'International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis dedicato alla ricerca condotta da team europei. Questo progetto ha preso forma in seguito al Congresso europeo di Antalya, in Turchia, nell'ottobre 2023.
 
È motivo di grande orgoglio vedere quanto sia vivace e dinamica la ricerca europea nel campo dell'ipnosi. L'Europa si sta affermando come protagonista. La diversità di talenti, la collaborazione tra nazioni e la varietà di progetti stanno consentendo alla ricerca europea di ampliare i confini della conoscenza. Questa è una vera fonte di ispirazione e ottimismo per il futuro. Un ringraziamento speciale a Gary Elkins, l'editore dell'International Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, e a Peter Naish, che hanno avuto un ruolo determinante nel dare vita a questo meraviglioso progetto. Gary delineerà i punti chiave di questo numero speciale in questa newsletter. Inoltre, questa newsletter è vostra e, tramite i nostri istituti, raggiunge migliaia di membri. Vi preghiamo di distribuirla e condividerla ampiamente. Siete sempre benvenuti qualora vogliate contribuire; in tal caso, sentitevi liberi di contattare Kathleen Long. Concluderò esprimendo la mia speranza che questa estate vi abbia permesso di ricaricarvi e che possiate iniziare questa nuova stagione con forza ed energia. 
Editor’s Letter KATHLEEN LONG

It’s officially autumn and in Scotland one of the great features at this time of year is the variety of colour changes that occur in the trees, mountains, valleys and surrounding lochs and rivers. Look up and you’ll see the first snow on the mountain peaks. This edition of the ESHNL , like the Scottish scenery, is full of variety. Our President Gerard Fitoussi reminds us that hope, determination and focus are crucial in helping our patients achieve their goals and what great examples of this were demonstrated in the recent Olympic Games in Paris. Gerard has also contributed once more to the French Corner. Fabio Carnevale, who is co-editor of the ESHNL, continues his exploration of AI, Maria Paola Brugnoli writes about rapid induction in emergency situations, Randi Abrahamsen updates on the progress of the ESH research project while handing over the chairmanship of CEPE to Andreas Kollar who also writes his first piece for the ESHNL. Geniveve Perennou contributes a very interesting article on adapting hypnosis for elderly with neuro-cognitive disorders, while Rachel Givati describes two cases in  children, one with OCD and another with school  refusal. No newsletter would be complete  without our regular research reflections from  Peter Naish and BSMDH have some pictures of  the happy new recruits on their annual hypnosis  foundation course. Andrea Di Ruvo takes us  through a presentation at the ISH Congress  Krakow while I contribute a short piece on the  International Festival of Storytelling in Vallendar. I  am delighted to highlight the special issue of a  joint collaboration between ESH and the  International Journal of Clinical and Experimental  Hypnosis presented by Gary Elkins PhD Editor in  chief of the IJCEH. The articles in 
the October 2024 edition are mainly from the ESH XVI  congress theme of “Hypnosis Food for Body and  Mind: An Integrated Approach to Healing” Th is i s hope fu l l y one o f many fu tu re collaborations. Our  Finnish Society invites us to celebrate their 65th  Jubilee with them which is quite a milestone in  anyone’s book. I would like to thank our  translators and all those who have contributed to  this autumn newsletter. Enjoy the bounty and color that autumn offers before winter steals  silently up on us.

Photo: K.L.
Did you know that your Constituent Society can apply to become an Accredited Training Society within ESH?
 
Your Constituent Society would need to provide training that would consist of Basic Theoretical Hypnosis Training of at least 50 hours and Supervision in Hypnosis applications of at least 50 hours.
 
Submission would require an application form to be completed along with content of training program, trainee criteria, trainee criteria & trainer CV’s and supervisor criteria and supervisor CV’s.
 
This would then all get sent for evaluation and approval by the Committee on Educational Programmes in Europe (CEPE). Once approved a certificate would be issued valid for three years.
 
If you would like anymore information please contact – mail@esh-hypnosis.eu 
Ideas From the World of Clinical Hypnosis

CLINICAL CASES 
18 
B.A. (Psychology) - from D.I.T. in Detroit Michigan (on the dean's list).  
M.A. - from the Hebrew University in Jerusalem (General Psychology, including various courses in educational Psych, clinical, and organizational psychology). About five years of specialization in psychological services:  Educational-Psychologist. 
Many years of experience in the school system, with school principals, teachers, students, and parents. (big part of that: As founder and manager of the psychological service). Many years of training and experience in stress and anxiety. At the same time, all these years in private practice (with children, teenagers, and adults). About the work I brought 
here: since most of my work was on the field only, I'm bringing here, two cases among the children and the youth that present difficulties, and the treatment is a little more complicated. The first one is with O.C.D and the second is with school phobia. 
Case Study 1 Presentation: A 10-Year-Old with Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder 
Case Overview: 
This case study examines a 10-year-old girl, referred to as Dana, who is experiencing Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder OCD. Dana is the daughter of a 42-year-old mother, who immigrated to Israel, from Russia, and works as a secretary at a college, and a 45-year-old father, who immigrated from Ukraine and works as a computer programmer. She also has a 7-year-old brother. Dana, a beautiful and charming fifth grader, accompanied her mother to the sessions. 
 
Presenting Issues:  
Dana's primary complaints included severe anxiety and numerous repetitive rituals. Examples of these rituals included spitting into a cup instead of swallowing, rubbing her foot on the floor, jumping, entering rooms with her right foot, and more. her anxiety, also manifested itself in great fear, when walking down the street and kept looking behind to see that there were no scary people there.
 
 
By Rachel Givati
Assessment and Methodology: 
Upon meeting Dana rapport was quickly established, and she was easy to communicate with. I employed a comprehensive Emotional-Behavioural-Social assessment, utilizing tools I developed over many years, alongside a detailed anamnesis. The assessment involved various tests, including:
 
- Extended human figure   drawing
 
- Sentence completion tasks
 
- Recollection of three happy memories and three sad memories
 
- A fairy tale exercise involving three gifts or wishes (tailored to the child's age)
 
- Direct or indirect conversation, depending on the child's developmental level
 
Therapeutic Approach: 
Based on the findings, I implemented a Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) approach with changing the status setting from negative to positive, and lots of positive reinforcement. The therapy aimed to enhance Dana's self-confidence and self-image. Hypnotic techniques were also integrated to manage anxiety and stress, including one hypnosis session towards the end of the process where Dana chose the "magic water" metaphor (out of options) "to wash away the "bad thoughts " while using "ego strengthening" technique, 
 
Parental Involvement: 
Guidance was provided to Dana's parents to support her progress at home. Their cooperation was crucial in focusing on the treatment and achieving positive outcomes. Notably, A consistently looked forward to 
and enjoyed her sessions, with her mother observing improvements and reductions in r i tual ist ic behaviours.
 
Outcome and Follow-Up: 
The treatment lasted approximately seven weeks, with weekly sessions. Progress was gradual, leading to a complete cessation of rituals and a significant anxiety reduction. It was discovered that Dana’s OCD symptoms were triggered by a distressing experience during a family trip to Madrid, where she witnessed a homeless drunk individual. This traumatic event was further exacerbated by a similar experience in Tel Aviv, leading to heightened fear and compulsive behaviour with many repetitions of rituals, (as mentioned above).
 
I would like to emphasize that, the fact that I had the cooperation of the child and her parents, contributed to a real success in treatment as described here,
 
With a sense of accomplishment, to me, and them!
 
Three months after the conclusion of treatment, Dana's mother reported that Dana was feeling very well, with no residual rituals or anxiety. This successful outcome underscores the importance of effective therapeutic strategies and strong parental involvement in managing and overcoming OCD.
 
Case Study 2 Presentation: Adolescent School Refusal and Family Dynamics 
Case Overview: 
A 14-year-old girl was referred to me by her father due to six months of school refusal. 
 
Background and Assessment: 
The girl is the youngest of two children (brother-17 years old), for an older pair of parents. the mother was diagnosed (according to the father) as" low " - on the general intellectual functioning level. Though well-meaning and caring, the parents struggled with effective communication with their daughter. This lack of communication led to significant anger and frustration from the girl.
 
Intervention: 
I began by providing a supportive space for the girl to express her frustrations and anger. The initial focus was on listening to her experiences and validating her feelings. Concurrently, efforts were made to bridge the communication gap between her and her parents. This involved helping the parents understand the generational differences and the importance of expressing their love more effectively.
 
Therapeutic Approach: 
In addition to the "CBT" approach, there was also, one session of Hypnosis, where the work there, was, Ego strengthening and experiencing the new effective tools, at home with the parents, and in the school in the academic and social areas.
 
Once the girl recognized that her parents cared for her, despite their communication challenges, we worked on enhancing her self-confidence. As her self-esteem improved, she gradually took on more responsibilities at home. Simultaneously, the parents were equipped with strategies to better understand their daughter's needs and to reinforce their parental authority.
 
Outcome: 
After approximately three months of therapy, the girl enrolled in a private high school began attending regularly, and achieved commendable academic 
p e r f o r m a n c e . T h e t re a t m e n t c o n c l u d e d successfully, and the parents later reported that she graduated from high school with success.
 
The process took about 4 months of a lot of effort… it finally brought good results. 
 
After about 2 months of continuous support and reinforcement, she showed good and stable results, which were demonstrated by positive performance and consistently good grades.
 
The all process took 6 months, and we finished with good feelings. 
 
Follow-Up: 
Approximately ten years later, I encountered the girl's father, who shared that she had married, settled in the northern region, and was happily living her life.
 
This case highlights the significance of addressing family communication issues and the impact of therapeutic interventions on improving both individual and family dynamics. 
ADAPTING HYPNOTHERAPY FOR ELDERLY INDIVIDUALS WITH NEUROCOGNITIVE DISORDERS 
21 
Trained in hypnosis by Michel Kerouac at the Milton Erickson Institute of Quebec, I have since adapted this technique to support elderly patients with dementia in hospitals and care facilities. This approach has proven particularly effective in alleviating anxiety and creating a more serene environment for care. By implementing hypnotic tools, we can significantly reduce distress in patients with dementia, enabling caregivers to provide treatment under much calmer conditions. This method requires careful adjustment to the specific needs of each individual, as patients may exhibit various behavioural disorders. The positive impact extends beyond the patients themselves, notably improving the experience for both caregivers and family members. To share these insights and offer practical solutions, I co authored a book with Serge Sirvain, published by Dunod, which provides guidance on applying this method in various care settings.   
Hypnosis and the elderly 
Various studies have demonstrated the effectiveness of the implementation of conversational hypnosis among seniors (Nightingale and Duff 2007. Wawrziczny et al., 2021, Bertoni 2020). This approach can be taught through short training courses that focus on:
 
The “pacing” technique, which connects to the person by meeting them where they are. This involves peaceful breathing, a reassuring posture, and a hypnogenic monotone voice. The “sprinkling” of soothing words to create a sense of security, (e.g., “peacefully”, “quietly”, “calmly”, “gently” …).
 
The creation of metaphors that can be integrated throughout the care process.
 
Training is most effective when conducted within a care unit, allowing all team members to collectively integrate these new skills (Perennou, 2017, 2024. Floccia, 2020, 2024). It is crucial to emphasize that this tool should only be used within each healthcare professional's area of expertise.
 
Hypnotic Tool: setting up a “Secure-Photography” intervention 
Due to neurodegenerative pathologies, many people can no longer name their places of emotional safety. It is necessary to identify those spaces upstream during informal conversations with the person or their relatives. This process should begin upon the resident's admission to the nursing home but can also be initiated by home care workers or family members. In a nursing home setting, this tool encourages staff to delve deeper into residents' life histories, uncovering previously unknown information about their past, profession, and interests. This research occurs naturally during initial encounters when the person arrives in the unit. During the initial set-up of the tool, the professionals of the unit distribute this work among themselves and posters of relevant information can be produced in a short time. Observation of the bedroom provides relevant information: Are there photos displayed other than those of grandchildren whom the elderly person may no longer recognize? Family photos should be be used cautiously, as nurses may not know the identity of each person or their relationship to the resident. 
 
By Genevieve Perennou
The photos will preferably be chosen when the patient is aged between ten and thirty because memory is generally more vivid and accessible in that period of life. The appropriateness of the chosen photos is systematically and spontaneously verified. Nurses, pleased with their completed posters, often share them in the staff room, where colleagues can readily recognize the resident depicted.
 
Implementing “Secure-Photography” 
The gathered information is then presented on an A4 sheet containing up to 4 photos. These often include images of the resident’s home, a significant journey, their occupation, animals, or a garden.... If the photo shows a specific place, it may be relevant to label it clearly so that all staff can correctly identify it: «the family home from 1950-1970, the farm, the village shop, the church of (name of) a village …”. These sheets are professionally presented and placed in the same location in all bedrooms within the nursing home. This allows each nurse to access the necessary information immediately upon entering the room. The care team thus has clear indications for directing the conversation, enabling them to adapt 
their communication approach with each elderly resident.
 
During care procedures, the nurse assists the resident in dissociating from the immediate situation, guiding him to settle into a metaphorical "protective bubble of comfort". This technique helps to reduce anxiety and discomfort associated with care activities.
 
Use of the Secure-Photography 
During care procedures, the induction of a trance state typically occurs within seconds through conversational hypnosis, as the elderly person with neuro-cognitive disorders is very often already in a dissociated state. S. Sirvain (2021) indicates that “many elderly patients with neurodegenerative pathologies are dissociated, in spontaneous or posttraumatic daily trance… Many patients, although physically present, appear to be elsewhere in their memories, absent from our reality and present. The eyes are turned inward. It looks like a presence/ absence, a dissociation.”
 
 
 
 
  By looking at these photographs, care staff can quickly iden'fy significant places or themes in the resident's life, such as Mont Saint-Michel, the seaside, or their former home. This informa'on provides healthcare professionals with several op'ons for crea'ng personalized metaphors to use during care procedures, enhancing the resident's comfort and engagement.
The caregiver selects one of the locations from the "Secure-Photography" sheet with which they feel comfortable creating a metaphor (Perennou, 2019). One nurse commented, “This tool in connection with the resident’s personalised life project allows us to pay special attention to each resident and thus to individualise, as much as possible, the treatment because too often in an institution, we tend to act in a systematic way” , said a nurse. Another nurse added, “Thanks to these two tools, “Secure-Photography” and conversat ional hypnosis, accompanying residents who initially refuse personal care has become much easier. Moreover, it has rekindled my passion for my job, which had become monotonous.” 
 
In case of hospitalisation 
An additional benefit of the Secure-Photography tool is its portability during hospital admissions. It can be included in the patient's transfer documentation, allowing hospital staff to display it in the room and thus have tailored conversation topics for each elderly patient with communication difficulties. This approach may help reduce wandering behaviour, the need for physical restraints, and potentially decrease the excessive administration of anxiolytic medications.
 
Some examples 
The Secure-Photography provides clues to help build metaphors and stories that the nurse can tell during bed baths or other care procedures (such as dressings, injections, etc.). For instance, a very aggressive lady who usually “casts spells” at the nurses had a pleasant experience when she was invited to imagine her cat joining her in bed. Throughout the process, the old lady cuddled her imaginary cat while the nurses talked about a “cat’s wash” (metaphor), saying, “as he passed his paw slightly wet to have comfortable fur… quietly… calmly” (sprinkling). The bed bath was completed peacefully for both the lady and the nurses. 
 
Another resident spoke quite frequently in a confused manner when discussing aeroplanes. He was said to have worked in naval aviation, according to the few known elements of his life history. During the bed bath, the nurse helped him imagine taking off in his plane, without knowing his exact function within the navy. She told him that the plane is in the clouds and the rain is falling, but the cockpit is not completely waterproof and that it is normal that he can feel a little water (metaphor), he is completely safe in his plane approaching the runway. The control tower has just asked you to “turn right and then left in order to land» (the nurse washed his back and changed the diaper). This metaphor continued until the resident was seated in his chair, with the nurse saying, “And there is a slight air hole that you know how to handle” when the hoist was used. The resident smiled at the end of the care, whereas he was usually disengaged.
 
Assessment 
To assess the effectiveness of this tool during challenging care situations, the team manager can establish specific targets in the nursing records. This allows analysing the specific behaviour of a patient over an extended period, as well as facilitating comparative analysis between units where this tool is employed and those where it is not.The physician can also monitor the reduction in sedative drug usage for individual residents, as well as compare medication utilisation across different units that have or have not integrated this tool.
 
Conclusion: Employing hypnosis techniques with elderly people who have cognitive impairments related to neurodegenerative diseases provides comfort for both residents and nurses. By developing tailored metaphors for the elderly, nurses can mobilise their creativity and caring values.This work can also form part of constructing the resident's individual care plan, reconnecting them with their life history. Involving the family in the search for relevant photographs makes them active participants in the care home admission process, which can often be emotionally challenging. 
Specificity of Trance in Elderly Individuals with Neurocognitive Disorders 
Hypnosis can be a valuable tool for managing behavioural disturbances and improving the quality of life in elderly individuals with neuro-cognitive disorders (NCDs). However, it is important to note that trance in these individuals’ presents certain peculiarities compared to trance in healthy adults.
 
Healthcare professionals working with moderate to severe cases may find themselves challenged by the phenomenon of intermittent trances. Patients with attentional disorders may spontaneously exit the trance state, potentially returning to it quickly. It is crucial to become accustomed to this oscillation between wakefulness and trance. The key here is the persistence of hypnotic discourse.
 
Due to these fluctuations, the trance level is often less profound, with less pronounced signs. While a trance state is typically characterized by decreased muscle tone, hyper-salivation, verbal deceleration, or spontaneous eye closure, in frail elderly individuals, one is more likely to observe a fixed gaze into space, reduced verbal production, or behavioural quiescence. As some individuals with NCDs may experience challenges in verbal communication, the hypnotherapist can utilize nonverbal communication techniques, such as touch and eye contact, to establish rapport with the individual and effectively convey instructions. Sessions tend to be shorter, though this does not necessarily indicate failure. The repetition of sessions throughout the day can be advantageous.
 
These trances, which occur without external human intervention, are most often reactive to situations of psychological stress, such as pain, confusion, and anxiety. It is therefore common to find the individual already in a trance state prior to any intervention. Identifying these moments is crucial, as they 
provide an entry point for hypnotic work. The objective is to "enter the patient's reality" in order to guide the trance using therapeutic suggestions. Negative hypnotic loops can thus be transformed into positive ones. 
 
References 
1. Duff, S.C and Nightingale, D.J (2007). Alternative Approaches to Supporting Individuals With Dementia: Enhancing Quality of Life Through H y p n o s i s , A l z h e i m e r ' s C a r e To d a y 8 ( 4 ) : 3 2 1 - 3 3 1 , DOI:10.1097/01.ALCAT.0000297153.89903.7b 2. Wawrziczny E., Buquet, A., Picard S., Archives of Gerontolgy ans Geriatrics, Volume 96, September-october 2021, 104453 3.  Bertoni P., Repères en Gériatrie, mai 2020, vol. 22, numéro 185 4.    Sirvain S., Repères en Gériatrie, mars 2021, vol. 23, numéro 193 « beaucoup de patients âgés atteints de pathologies neurodégénératives sont dissociés, en transe quotidienne spontanée ou post-traumatique… Nombre de patients, bien que présents physiquement, semblent ailleurs dans leurs souvenirs, absents de notre réalité et de notre présent. Les regards sont tournés vers une intériorité. Cela ressemble bien à une présence/absence, à une dissociation. » 5.    Perennou G., L’hypnose pour accompagner les patients âgés, Bruxelles, Satas, 2017 6.  Perennou G., Métaphores hypnotiques pour accompagner les patients lors des soins techniques, Bruxelles, Satas, 2019 7. Perennou G., Sirvain S., Hypnose médicale de la personne âgée pour les professionnels de la santé. Dunod, 2024 8. Floccia M., Hypnoses en pratique gériatrique, Dunod, 2020 9. Floccia M., Cas pratiques en hypnose gériatrique, Dunod, 2024 
HYPNOSIS, AI AND GENERATIVE TECHNOLOGIES 
25 
The continuous innovations involving the world of AI are disrupting al l fields of culture, work, entertainment, and science. Their improvement is accelerating fast, and every day, there are more things it can do better than us. There are risks, but the opportunities for human society are  enormous. 
 
This means that the world of psychotherapy, clinics, and medicine - even more so,  hypnosis applied to these fields - needs continuous updating and deep reflection on the impact of  these tools  on both hypnotherapy clinicians and their patients. 
 
This series of articles dedicated to the  revolution that AI is imposing on the world of science aims precisely to stimulate through  examples, information, and metaphors a complex reflection on what impact this type of  technology is having and may have on the field of psychotherapy and hypnotic psychotherapy. 
 
One of the most exciting questions may concern the impact of how we build and maintain rapport with our hypnosis patients in the future. In this regard, an old example can generate several extraordinarily fertile implications. 
 
It concerns the very famous game 'GO.' Go is an ancient strategy board game originating in China. Its history dates back over 2,500 years,  making it one of the oldest games still played. According to 
legend, the Chinese emperor Yao  invented it to teach his son the principles of wisdom and discipline. Other accounts suggest that  generals used it to simulate battles and develop strategic skills. 
 
Over the centuries, Go spread throughout East Asia, reaching Korea and Japan around the 7th  century. In Japan, the game became extremely popular, and a true cultural tradition developed  with the creation of specialized schools and the organization of tournaments. Japanese Go  peaked during the Edo period (1603-1868) when an official position of 'Meijin' (Grand Master of  Go) was established. In the 20th century, Go also began to gain popularity in the West, thanks to cultural exchanges  and the interest of game scholars. 
 
Go organizations and federations were founded in various  countries, and the game became an international activity with world tournaments. Well, artificial intelligence (AI) revolutionized the theories and practices of the ancient game of Go in profound ways, introducing new strategies and innovative approaches and transforming the landscape of this game. This revolution had its most significant symbolic moment in 2016, when AlphaGo, a program developed by DeepMind (an AI research company owned by Google),  defeated Lee Sedol, one of the best Go players in the world.  
 
 
By Fabio Carnevale
This event demonstrated that AI could surpass human capabilities in a game considered among  the most complex due to its vastness of possible combinations and its need for strategic intuition. BUT above all, AI invented and applied ways of playing that no human had ever conceived in over  2000 years. 
 
AlphaGo and subsequent AI programs, such as AlphaZero, developed and introduced  unexpected strategies that challenged the traditional principles of Go. AI led to the discovery of  new moves and sequences that initially seemed counterintuitive to human players but proved  highly effective. For example, according to classical game theory, A lphaGo prefer red moves that  seemed unconventional or weak but proved to be winning in the long run. These approaches  forced human players to reconsider their notions of opening and territorial control, leading to  greater game experimentation and innovation. 
 
There's more. Let me give to you another example: 'The Last Screenwriter" is the first film entirely written by artificial intelligence. Its premiere, scheduled for last June in London, was canceled due to the numerous controversies that were arising. 
 
The film, directed by Peter Luisi, concerns the story of a screenwriter who realizes he is a less gifted screenwriter than artificial intelligence. A sort of meta-film. 
 
To understand the significance of these themes, I propose three extraordinary examples of the application of AI in the field of human sciences and medicine. Among the most recent is Regina Barzilay from MIT ( Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Cambridge, USA) . 
 
After her personal experience of breast cancer she created a significant  breakthrough in detecting early-stage breast cancer by AI. She also led the 
team that used  machine learning to discover Halicin, the first new antibiotic in 30 years. 
 
David Silver is a Principal Research Scientist at Google DeepMind. He led the AlphaGo team that built the AI to defeat the world's best human Go player through sophisticated reinforcement learning processes. AIs use these processes to learn new concepts in a way that is completely  similar to how animals and humans do but with an infinitely faster learning speed. 
 
The exciting thing is that, since you cannot give a computer a reinforcement similar to that used  for a dog or a dolphin (a cookie or a fish) or a human (a good grade or a compliment), to reinforce  the correct answers, machine learning occurs through a suitable algorithm or a lousy algorithm,  which have the value of OK or not OK. 
 
Paolo Pirjanian founded Embodied and is a pioneer in developing emotionally intelligent robots to  aid child development. The goal of AIs capable of distinguishing human emotions has led  Pirjanian's team to prPirjanian's capable of training social skills in healthy human subjects and  subjects diagnosed within the autism spectrum. These robots train young humans in eye contact and turn-taking skills in complex social  relationships. 
 
If you think these topics are science fiction or irrelevant, you just need to check how many publications there are constantly on PubMed regarding the use of AI in medicine, psychiatry, and psychotherapy to understand the significance of the revolution underway. 
 
On the other hand, these tools continue to improve constantly through reinforcement learning  processes that occur 24 hours a day, seven days a week. This means their capabilities will have  already improved since you started reading this article. 
The fact that these tools are evolving so quickly leads to a first dilemma: our ability to understand their significance and imagine their use is slower and requires more time for reflection to integrate them into our daily practices. 
 
One of the most recent and interesting articles on this topic is "Quality and correctness of AI generated versus human-written abstracts in psychiatric research papers" published this year by Tien-Wei Hsu and colleagues in the journal " P s y c h i a t r y R e s e a r c h " h t t p s : / / pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39213714/ The article evaluated the ability of an artificial intelligence  
 
(AI)-based chatbot to generate abstracts from academic psychiatric articles. Five experts 
evaluated the quality of the 40 abstracts (about half of which were written by the AI)  using a blinded approach. 
 
The detected AI content was 31.48% for generated structured abstracts, 75.58% for unstructured abstracts, and 66.48% for active comparators' abstracts. 
 
For quality, generated structured  abstracts were rated similarly to originals, but unstructured ones received significantly lower  scores. Experts rated 40% accuracy with structured abstracts, 73% with unstructured ones, and  77% for active comparators. However, 30% of AI-generated abstract conclusions were incorrect.

By Andreas Kollar
Dear colleagues and members of the European Society of Hypnosis,
 
I am deeply honored to assume the role of Chair for the Committee on Educational Programmes in Europe (CEPE). I am taking over from Randi Abrahamson, who has been instrumental in advancing our mission and will now focus her efforts on a core function within ESH's important research project evaluating the efficacy of hypnosis..
 
Our work at CEPE focuses on several key initiatives, including the accreditation of training societies (ATS) and the European Society of Hypnosis Certificate (ESHC). These programs are designed to ensure that hypnosis education maintains a high level of quality and integrity, offering practitioners the tools they need to excel in their field. Additionally, we aim to inspire societies to contribute to our shared training database, which serves as a resource to elevate educational standards across Europe.
 
As I step into this position, I am committed to continuing the excellent work already underway. Together with my colleagues, we will work to expand the reach of our certification programs, simplify application processes, and promote innovative educational tools, such as our growing collection of videos and workshops. I am confident that, through these efforts, we will support both emerging and experienced professionals in their pursuit of excellence in hypnosis.
 
I look forward to collaborating with all of you to further strengthen the educational foundation of hypnosis in Europe.

Research Reflections 
31 
Burkhard Peter (2024) has published a very interesting paper that he has titled Hypnosis in psychotherapy, psychosomatics and medicine. A brief overview. I’m not sure that the title does this article justice. It is effectively a history of hypnosis, but not one of those boring, ‘yet another one’ accounts! This one assesses the theories and practices in the context of the prevailing philosophies of the time and also evaluates them against more modern ideas. At 12 pages (not counting the copious references) it is not, as the title suggests, particularly brief, but it is a rewarding read. Rather than attempt a summary I will just follow through one of the issues it raised for me.
 
Burkhard makes a distinction between the clinical use of hypnosis and its scientific investigation, although he does recommend more research studies (science) to evaluate the use of hypnosis in the medical field. The science/therapy distinction is very much tied to the issue of susceptibility. Therapists generally have neither the time nor, to be honest, the inclination to evaluate a patient’s hypnotisability. I have mentioned in these pages before that it is striking that so many clinicians have the impression that their clients don’t seem to include the non-susceptible. Where have the ‘lows’ gone? Is it that the scientists (who do see hypnotic lows) are actually working with a different process? This seems to me to be very unlikely. A more 
plausible explanation, I think, is that the anxiety involved (especially in a discipline such as dentistry) assists hypnosis by causing a shift in activity towards the right hemisphere (e.g. Abrahamsen & Naish, 2021).
 
Burkhard himself comments upon the weak impact of hypnotic susceptibility upon therapeutic success rates, pointing out that many studies have used relatively few patients, an insufficient number to demonstrate strong statistical significance. This is true, but one could argue the reverse. In other words, if it takes a large number of people to demonstrate the facilitating effect of having higher hypnotic susceptibility, then perhaps this means that the advantage is very small – which is exactly what the studies show. There appear to be two possible explanations: either there is no need to be very hypnotisable to gain therapeutic benefit, or the presence of people of lower susceptibility, who, through hemispheric shifts have temporarily become rather higher, means that there is no longer a clear spread of susceptibility. This is in contrast to a scientist testing in a laboratory, where there is no hemispheric shift to blur the distinction.
 
It would be nice if it were possible to have a simple and quick way of enhancing people’s susceptibility, and thereby produce a clearer, stronger link with favourable outcomes. With this in mind I was 
 
By Peter Naish
interested to find a paper by David White and colleagues (White, Ciorciari, Carbis & Liley; 2009) an Australian group. They used virtual reality (VR) headsets with their participants, which delivered natural sounds and images (e.g. of rivers) while a spoken induction and suggestions were being given. In principle this sounds helpful, because it might assist those who struggle to generate rich imagery of their own. The authors believed that the VR was likely to boost the responsiveness of the lower scorers. 
 
They do not mention the spread of hypnotisability across participants, but they do say that, rather than preselecting highs and lows, they merely split the participants into two, about the mid-range score, then compared the higher- and lower-scoring groups Unfortunately, most of the paper reports the impact of this hypnosis upon the EEG signals picked up by scalp electrodes, rather than exploring the specific contribution of the VR. There was a clear effect of hypnosis, showing that the VR technique worked, but not whether it was better than traditional hypnosis.
 
We do know that VR alone can be used therapeutically. For example some success has been found in using immersive VR to treat soldiers suffering from PTSD. This form of VR presents a 360° world and allows movement. In that way a soldier can feel that he is advancing (in reality he is walking on a treadmill) into battle, with all the expected explosions etc. 
 
However, hypnosis can be used for PTSD too, and it can generate all the same realistic effects (via 
hallucinations) as VR, but without the expensive equipment. What we have lacked is a study linking the two. There is a paper which reviews studies to date, in press in the IJCEH, by Mélanie Louras and a large team of collaborators. The paper does not confine itself to hypnosis, but explores the combination of VR with a range of ‘mind-body’ therapies, i.e. mindfulness, relaxation, meditation and hypnosis. 
 
Curiously, hypnosis appears to be the only therapy that is not enhanced by the addition of VR. Perhaps this is because it is the only therapy that can generate its own virtual reality.
 
Happy Hypnotising!
 
References 
Abrahamsen, R. & Naish, P. (2021). Studies in patients with temporomandibular disorders pain: Can scales of hypnotic susceptibility predict the outcome on pain relief? American J. of Clinical H y p n o s i s , 6 4 ( 1 ) , 1 2 - 1 9 , D O I : 10.1080/00029157.2020.1863183 
 
Louras, M. et al. (2024). Virtual reality combined with mind-body therapies for the management of pain: a scoping review. Intl. J. of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, (in press).
 
White, D., Ciorciari, J., Carbis, C. & Liley, D. (2009). EEG correlates of virtual reality hypnosis. Intl. J. of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, 57(1), 94-116, DOI: 10.1080/00207140802463690

We are very grateful to the people, who contributed with work to expand the project so far. We also have contact with the Belgian and Swedish society.
 
An application for approval of the project by the ethics committee at Vienna University was sent with the help of professor emeritus Henriette Walther. We are waiting for the answer.
 
The MYMOP questionnaire is used in the ESH project. When we started using this questionnaire it was a free tool, but ESH now has to pay a fee for the use and we are in the process of being approved to use it.
